I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` solutions to test, each solution `i` needs `a_i` tests (taking 1 second per test)
2. There are `k` parallel testing processes
3. Solutions are tested in order from 1 to n
4. At any time, when a process is free, it takes the next solution from the queue
5. For any time moment `t`, if `m` solutions are fully tested by time `t`, the caption shows "System testing: d%" where `d = round(100 * m / n)`
6. A solution is "interesting" if there exists a time when it's being tested on test `q` and the caption shows "q%"

## Key Insights

1. We need to simulate the testing process to determine when each solution starts and finishes
2. For each solution, we need to find if there's a moment when it's being tested on test `q` and the caption shows exactly `q%`
3. The caption shows percentage `d%` when there are `m` fully tested solutions (where `d = round(100 * m / n)`)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Simulate the testing process**: Use a priority queue to track when each testing process becomes free
2. **For each solution**: Determine when it starts and when it finishes
3. **For each time moment**: Determine how many solutions are fully tested
4. **Check if any solution is interesting**: For each solution, check if during any time when it's being tested on test `q`, the caption shows `q%`

## Detailed Approach

1. Use a min-heap to track when each testing process becomes free
2. Process solutions in order, assigning each to the first available process
3. For each solution, record its start time and end time
4. For each solution, iterate through each test it's being tested on
5. For each test time, calculate the number of fully tested solutions and the caption percentage
6. Check if at any moment during testing on test `q`, the caption shows exactly `q%`

```python
import heapq
from math import floor

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Simulate the testing process
    # heap stores (free_time, process_id)
    free_times = [0] * k
    # Process the solutions in order
    start_time = [0] * n
    end_time = [0] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest free time among all processes
        min_time = min(free_times)
        # Find which process is free earliest
        process_idx = free_times.index(min_time)
        
        # Solution i starts at min_time
        start_time[i] = min_time
        
        # The solution ends at start_time + a[i]
        end_time[i] = start_time[i] + a[i]
        
        # Update the free time of that process
        free_times[process_idx] = end_time[i]
    
    # For each solution, check if it's interesting
    interesting = 0
    
    # We'll check at each time moment when a solution is being tested on a specific test
    # For each solution, go through each test it's being tested on
    for i in range(n):
        for test in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            # The solution is being tested on test 'test' at time (start_time[i] + test - 1)
            current_time = start_time[i] + test - 1
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by current_time
            m = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if end_time[j] <= current_time:
                    m += 1
            
            # Calculate the caption percentage
            d = round(100 * m / n)
            
            # If we're testing on test q and the caption shows q%, then it's interesting
            if d == test:
                interesting += 1
                break  # Once we find one such moment, this solution is interesting
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the percentage calculation. Let me reconsider.

The percentage is calculated as `round(100 * m / n)`, which is equivalent to `floor(100 * m / n + 0.5)`.

Let me verify with the examples:

For the first example: n=2, a=[49,100]
- Process 1: solution 1 from 0 to 49
- Process 2: solution 2 from 0 to 100

At time 49.5 (testing solution 2 on test 50):
- Solutions fully tested: only solution 1 (end_time=49)
- m = 1, so d = round(100 * 1 / 2) = round(50) = 50
- We're testing on test 50, and caption shows 50%, so it's interesting.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya likes taking part in Codeforces contests. When a round is over, Vasya follows all submissions in the system testing tab.

There are $n$ solutions, the $i$-th of them should be tested on $a_i$ tests, testing one solution on one test takes $1$ second. The solutions are judged in the order from $1$ to $n$. There are $k$ testing processes which test solutions simultaneously. Each of them can test at most one solution at a time.

At any time moment $t$ when some testing process is not judging any solution, it takes the first solution from the queue and tests it on each test in increasing order of the test ids. Let this solution have id $i$, then it is being tested on the first test from time moment $t$ till time moment $t + 1$, then on the second test till time moment $t + 2$ and so on. This solution is fully tested at time moment $t + a_i$, and after that the testing process immediately starts testing another solution.

Consider some time moment, let there be exactly $m$ fully tested solutions by this moment. There is a caption "System testing: $d$%" on the page with solutions, where $d$ is calculated as

$$d = round\left(100\cdot\frac{m}{n}\right),$$

where $round(x) = \lfloor{x + 0.5}\rfloor$ is a function which maps every real to the nearest integer.

Vasya calls a submission interesting if there is a time moment (possibly, non-integer) when the solution is being tested on some test $q$, and the caption says "System testing: $q$%". Find the number of interesting solutions.

Please note that in case when multiple processes attempt to take the first submission from the queue at the same moment (for instance, at the initial moment), the order they take the solutions does not matter.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two positive integers $n$ and $k$ ($1 \le n \le 1000$, $1 \le k \le 100$) standing for the number of submissions and the number of testing processes respectively.

The second line contains $n$ positive integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 150$), where $a_i$ is equal to the number of tests the $i$-th submission is to be run on.


-----Output-----

Output the only integer — the number of interesting submissions.


-----Examples-----
Input
2 2
49 100

Output
1

Input
4 2
32 100 33 1

Output
2

Input
14 5
48 19 6 9 50 20 3 42 38 43 36 21 44 6

Output
5



-----Note-----

Consider the first example. At time moment $0$ both solutions start testing. At time moment $49$ the first solution is fully tested, so at time moment $49.5$ the second solution is being tested on the test $50$, and the caption says "System testing: $50$%" (because there is one fully tested solution out of two). So, the second solution is interesting.

Consider the second example. At time moment $0$ the first and the second solutions start testing. At time moment $32$ the first solution is fully tested, the third solution starts testing, the caption says "System testing: $25$%". At time moment $32 + 24.5 = 56.5$ the third solutions is being tested on test $25$, the caption is still the same, thus this solution is interesting. After that the third solution is fully tested at time moment $32 + 33 = 65$, the fourth solution is fully tested at time moment $65 + 1 = 66$. The captions becomes "System testing: $75$%", and at time moment $74.5$ the second solution is being tested on test $75$. So, this solution is also interesting. Overall, there are two interesting solutions.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` solutions that need to be tested, with solution `i` requiring `a_i` tests
2. There are `k` testing processes that work simultaneously
3. Solutions are processed in order from 1 to n
4. Each process can only test one solution at a time
5. When a solution is being tested on test `q`, we check if the system testing caption shows "q%"
6. The caption shows "d%" where `d = round(100 * m / n)` and `m` is the number of fully tested solutions at that moment
7. A solution is "interesting" if at any moment while it's being tested on test `q`, the caption shows "q%"

## Key Insights

1. We need to simulate the timeline of when each solution starts and completes testing
2. For each solution, we need to find if there exists a time when it's being tested on test `q` such that at that time, exactly `q` solutions have been fully tested (rounded to nearest integer)
3. The caption shows "q%" when exactly `q` solutions are fully tested

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Simulate the testing schedule**: Determine when each solution starts and finishes
2. **Track when solutions complete**: We need to know the completion time of each solution
3. **Check each solution's testing phase**: For each solution, check each test it's being tested on, and at that time, verify if the caption would show the same percentage

Let me work through the timeline:

- We have `k` processes, so solutions are distributed among them
- We need to simulate the start times of each solution
- We can use a priority queue to simulate the process

For each solution `i`, it starts at `start_time[i]` and finishes at `start_time[i] + a_i`.

For a test `q` on solution `i`, the time is `start_time[i] + q - 1`.

At that time, we need to count how many solutions have finished by that time.

## Algorithm

1. Simulate the process to find when each solution starts
2. For each solution and each test it goes through, check if at that time the caption shows the same percentage as the test number
3. Count how many solutions satisfy this condition

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find when each solution starts testing
    # Use a min-heap to track when processes become free
    process_free_times = [0] * k  # free time of each process
    
    start_times = [0] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest free time among all processes
        earliest_free = min(process_free_times)
        # Assign solution i to a process that becomes free earliest
        # We can use any process, so we just take the minimum
        start_times[i] = earliest_free
        
        # Update the free time of that process
        # Find which process was freed earliest
        min_idx = process_free_times.index(earliest_free)
        process_free_times[min_idx] = start_times[i] + a[i]
    
    # Now check each solution and each test it goes through
    interesting = 0
    
    # For each solution
    for i in range(n):
        solution_start = start_times[i]
        
        # For each test q (1-indexed) of this solution
        for q in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            # Time when solution i is being tested on test q
            test_time = solution_start + q
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by test_time
            completed_count = 0
            for j in range(n):
                # Solution j is completed at start_times[j] + a[j]
                if start_times[j] + a[j] <= test_time:
                    completed_count += 1
            
            # Calculate the caption percentage
            m = completed_count
            caption_percent = round(100 * m / n)
            
            # Check if this matches the test number q
            if caption_percent == q:
                interesting += 1
                break  # No need to check further tests for this solution
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Simulate the start times using a min-heap
    # Heap will store (free_time, solution_index)
    # But we don't need to track index, just the free times
    free_times = [0] * k
    start_times = [0] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest free time
        earliest_free = min(free_times)
        # Assign this solution to a process that becomes free earliest
        # We can update any process, so we just keep track of the minimum
        start_times[i] = earliest_free
        
        # Update the free time of the process that just finished
        # Find which process was free earliest
        min_idx = free_times.index(earliest_free)
        free_times[min_idx] = start_times[i] + a[i]
    
    # Check each solution and each test
    interesting = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        start_time = start_times[i]
        for test_num in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            time = start_time + test_num
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by this time
            completed = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if start_times[j] + a[j] <= time:
                    completed += 1
            
            # Calculate caption percentage
            caption_percent = round(100 * completed / n)
            
            # Check if it matches the test number
            if caption_percent == test_num:
                interesting += 1
                break
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Simulate the start times using a min-heap
    # Heap will store (free_time, solution_index)
    # But we don't need to track index, just the free times
    free_times = [0] * k

        # We can update any process, so we just keep track of the minimum
        start_times[i] = earliest_free

        # Find which process was free earliest
        min_idx = free_times.index(earliest_free)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` solutions to test, each solution `i` needs `a_i` tests (taking 1 second per test)
2. There are `k` parallel testing processes
3. Solutions are processed in order from 1 to n
4. When a process finishes a solution, it immediately takes the next available solution
5. At any time, the percentage shown is `round(100 * m / n)` where `m` is the number of fully tested solutions
6. A solution is "interesting" if at some moment when it's being tested on test `q`, the percentage shown is exactly `q%`

## Key Insights

1. We need to simulate the testing process to track when each solution starts and finishes
2. For each solution, we need to check if there's a moment when it's being tested on test `q` (where `q` is between 1 and `a_i`) and at that exact moment, the percentage displayed is `q%`
3. The percentage at time `t` is determined by how many solutions have been fully tested by time `t`

## Solution Steps

1. Simulate the testing process using a priority queue (min-heap) to track when each process becomes free
2. For each solution, calculate when it starts testing and when it finishes
3. For each test within a solution, check if at that moment the percentage displayed equals the test number
4. Count how many solutions have at least one "interesting" moment

Let me implement this:

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Priority queue to store when each process becomes free
    # (finish_time, process_id)
    process_times = []
    
    # For each solution, store when it starts and when it ends
    start_times = [0] * n
    end_times = [0] * n
    
    # Initialize k processes available at time 0
    for _ in range(k):
        heapq.heappush(process_times, 0)
    
    # Process each solution in order
    for i in range(n):
        # Get the earliest available time for a process
        available_time = heapq.heappop(process_times)
        
        # This solution starts at available_time
        start_time = available_time
        end_time = start_time + a[i]
        
        # Record start and end times
        start_times[i] = start_time
        end_times[i] = end_time
        
        # Add the end time back to the process queue
        heapq.heappush(process_times, end_time)
    
    # Now check each solution for interesting moments
    interesting = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        # For each test within this solution
        for test_num in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            # The time when solution i is being tested on test test_num
            test_time = start_times[i] + test_num
            
            # Calculate how many solutions are fully tested by time test_time
            # A solution j is fully tested by time test_time if end_times[j] <= test_time
            fully_tested_count = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if end_times[j] <= test_time:
                    fully_tested_count += 1
            
            # Calculate the percentage shown
            percentage = round(100 * fully_tested_count / n)
            
            # Check if this matches the test number
            if percentage == test_num:
                interesting += 1
                break  # No need to check other tests for this solution
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

```python
    # Current solution starts at available_time, finishes at available_time + a[i]
```

```python
    start_times.append(start_time)

    for test_num in range(1, a[i] + 1):

        fully_tested_count = 0
        for j in range(n):
            if end_times[j] <= test_time:
                fully_tested_count += 1
        
        # Calculate percentage
        percentage = round(100 * fully_tested_count / n)
        
        # Check if it matches the test number
        if percentage == test_num:
            interesting += 1
            break  # Once we find one interesting moment, we're done for this solution

print(interesting)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
import heapq

n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Priority queue to store when each process becomes free
process_times = []
for _ in range(k):
    heapq.heappush(process_times, 0)

# Store start and end times for each solution
start_times = []
end_times = []

# Process each solution in order
for i in range(n):
    # Get the earliest available time
    available_time = heapq.heappop(process_times)
    
    # Current solution starts at available_time, finishes at available_time + a[i]
    start_time = available_time
    finish_time = start_time + a[i]
    
    start_times.append(start_time)
    end_times.append(finish_time)
    
    # Add the finish time back to the process queue
    heapq.heappush(process_times, finish_time)

# Check each solution for interesting moments
interesting = 0

for i in range(n):
    # For each test within this solution
    for test_num in range(1, a[i] + 1):
        # Time when solution i is being tested on test test_num
        test_time = start_times[i] + test_num
        
        # Calculate how many solutions are fully tested by time test_time
        fully_tested_count = 0
        for j in range(n):
            if end_times[j] <= test_time:
                fully_tested_count += 1
        
        # Calculate percentage
        percentage = round(100 * fully_tested_count / n)
        
        # Check if it matches the test number
        if percentage == test_num:
            interesting += 1
            break  # Once we find one interesting moment, we're done for this solution

print(interesting)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya likes taking part in Codeforces contests. When a round is over, Vasya follows all submissions in the system testing tab.

There are $n$ solutions, the $i$-th of them should be tested on $a_i$ tests, testing one solution on one test takes $1$ second. The solutions are judged in the order from $1$ to $n$. There are $k$ testing processes which test solutions simultaneously. Each of them can test at most one solution at a time.

At any time moment $t$ when some testing process is not judging any solution, it takes the first solution from the queue and tests it on each test in increasing order of the test ids. Let this solution have id $i$, then it is being tested on the first test from time moment $t$ till time moment $t + 1$, then on the second test till time moment $t + 2$ and so on. This solution is fully tested at time moment $t + a_i$, and after that the testing process immediately starts testing another solution.

Consider some time moment, let there be exactly $m$ fully tested solutions by this moment. There is a caption "System testing: $d$%" on the page with solutions, where $d$ is calculated as

$$d = round\left(100\cdot\frac{m}{n}\right),$$

where $round(x) = \lfloor{x + 0.5}\rfloor$ is a function which maps every real to the nearest integer.

Vasya calls a submission interesting if there is a time moment (possibly, non-integer) when the solution is being tested on some test $q$, and the caption says "System testing: $q$%". Find the number of interesting solutions.

Please note that in case when multiple processes attempt to take the first submission from the queue at the same moment (for instance, at the initial moment), the order they take the solutions does not matter.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two positive integers $n$ and $k$ ($1 \le n \le 1000$, $1 \le k \le 100$) standing for the number of submissions and the number of testing processes respectively.

The second line contains $n$ positive integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 150$), where $a_i$ is equal to the number of tests the $i$-th submission is to be run on.


-----Output-----

Output the only integer — the number of interesting submissions.


-----Examples-----
Input
2 2
49 100

Output
1

Input
4 2
32 100 33 1

Output
2

Input
14 5
48 19 6 9 50 20 3 42 38 43 36 21 44 6

Output
5



-----Note-----

Consider the first example. At time moment $0$ both solutions start testing. At time moment $49$ the first solution is fully tested, so at time moment $49.5$ the second solution is being tested on the test $50$, and the caption says "System testing: $50$%" (because there is one fully tested solution out of two). So, the second solution is interesting.

Consider the second example. At time moment $0$ the first and the second solutions start testing. At time moment $32$ the first solution is fully tested, the third solution starts testing, the caption says "System testing: $25$%". At time moment $32 + 24.5 = 56.5$ the third solutions is being tested on test $25$, the caption is still the same, thus this solution is interesting. After that the third solution is fully tested at time moment $32 + 33 = 65$, the fourth solution is fully tested at time moment $65 + 1 = 66$. The captions becomes "System testing: $75$%", and at time moment $74.5$ the second solution is being tested on test $75$. So, this solution is also interesting. Overall, there are two interesting solutions.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` solutions that need to be tested, with solution `i` requiring `a_i` tests
2. There are `k` testing processes that work simultaneously
3. Solutions are processed in order from 1 to n
4. Each process can only work on one solution at a time
5. At any time, when a process is free, it takes the next available solution from the queue
6. A solution is "interesting" if there exists a time when:
   - The solution is being tested on test `q` (so at time `t + q - 1` where `t` is when it started)
   - At that exact time, the system caption shows "System testing: `q`%"
   - The caption shows `q%` when there are exactly `q` fully tested solutions out of `n`

## Key Insights

1. The caption shows `d%` where `d = round(100 * m / n)` and `m` is the number of fully tested solutions at time `t`
2. For solution `i` to be interesting at time `t` when it's being tested on test `q`, we need:
   - `m = q` (the number of fully tested solutions equals the test number being executed)
   - The time when solution `i` is being tested on test `q` is `start_time + q - 1`
   - At that time, exactly `q` solutions are fully tested

## Solution Approach

1. Simulate the testing process to determine when each solution starts and finishes
2. For each solution, track when it starts being tested
3. For each solution, check if there's any time when it's being tested on test `q` such that exactly `q` solutions are fully tested at that time
4. A solution is interesting if such a situation occurs

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Use a priority queue to simulate the testing process - track when each process becomes free
2. For each solution, determine its start time (when the first free process becomes available)
3. For each solution, we need to check if at any point during its testing (on test `q`), the number of fully tested solutions equals `q`
4. The number of fully tested solutions at time `t` is the number of solutions that have finished by time `t`

Let me implement this:

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Priority queue to track when each testing process becomes free
    # (time, process_id)
    free_times = [0] * k  # free_times[i] = time when process i becomes free
    
    # Track when each solution starts being tested
    start_time = [0] * n
    
    # Simulate the testing process
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest time when a process becomes free
        min_free_time = min(free_times)
        start_time[i] = min_free_time
        
        # Update the free time of the process that was freed earliest
        process_idx = free_times.index(min_free_time)
        free_times[process_idx] = start_time[i] + a[i]
    
    # Now check for each solution if it's interesting
    interesting_count = 0
    
    # For each solution i, we need to check if there's a test q such that:
    # At time (start_time[i] + q - 1), exactly q solutions are fully tested
    
    # First, we need to determine the finish times of all solutions
    finish_times = [start_time[i] + a[i] for i in range(n)]
    
    # For each solution, check all possible test numbers q (from 1 to a[i])
    for i in range(n):
        # Check each test q (from 1 to a[i])
        for q in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            time_when_testing_q = start_time[i] + q - 1
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by this time
            m = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if finish_times[j] <= time_when_testing_q:
                    m += 1
            
            # Check if at this time, exactly q solutions are fully tested
            if m == q:
                interesting_count += 1
                break  # Once we find one such q, the solution is interesting
    
    return interesting_count

print(solve())
```

- n=2, k=2, a=[49,100]
- Both processes start at time 0
- Solution 1 finishes at 49, Solution 2 finishes at 100
- At time 49.5, solution 2 is being tested on test 50 (since it started at 0, at time 49.5 it has been testing for 49.5 seconds)
- At time 49.5, how many solutions are fully tested? Only solution 1 (finish time 49 ≤ 49.5), so m=1
- So at time 49.5, caption shows 100 * 1 / 2 = 50%, which is 50%

"d = round(100 * m / n)" where m is the number of fully tested solutions.

We need a time when the solution is being tested on test q, and the caption shows q%.

So we need: d = q, where d = round(100 * m / n)

This means: round(100 * m / n) = q

So 100 * m / n should round to q.

For the first example at time 49.5:
- m = 1 (only solution 1 is fully tested)
- d = round(100 * 1 / 2) = round(50) = 50
- We're testing on test q = 50
- So d = 50 and q = 50, so this works.

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Priority queue to track when each testing process becomes free
    # We'll use a min-heap for free times
    free_times = [0] * k
    
    # Track when each solution starts being tested
    start_time = [0] * n
    
    # Simulate the testing process
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest free time
        min_free_time = min(free_times)
        start_time[i] = min_free_time
        
        # Update the free time of the process that was freed earliest
        # Find which process to assign to this solution
        for j in range(k):
            if free_times[j] == min_free_time:
                free_times[j] = start_time[i] + a[i]
                break
    
    # Calculate finish times for each solution
    finish_times = [start_time[i] + a[i] for i in range(n)]
    
    # Check each solution to see if it's interesting
    interesting_count = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Check each test q from 1 to a[i]
        for q in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            time_when_testing_q = start_time[i] + q - 1
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by this time
            m = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if finish_times[j] <= time_when_testing_q:
                    m += 1
            
            # Calculate the caption percentage
            d = round(100 * m / n)
            
            # If the caption shows q%, then the solution is interesting
            if d == q:
                interesting_count += 1
                break  # Found one interesting moment for this solution
    
    return interesting_count

print(solve())
```

def solve():

    a = list(map(int, input().split()))

    # We'll use a min-heap for free times
    free_times = [0] * k

    start_time = [0] * n

        min_free_time = min(free_times)
        start_time[i] = min_free_time

        # Find which process to assign to this solution
        for j in range(k):
            if free_times[j] == min_free_time:
                free_times[j] = start_time[i] + a[i]
                break

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` solutions to test, each solution `i` needs `a_i` tests (takes `a_i` seconds to complete)
2. There are `k` testing processes that work simultaneously
3. Solutions are processed in order from 1 to n
4. When a process is free, it takes the next solution from the queue
5. The system shows a percentage: `d = round(100 * m / n)` where `m` is the number of fully tested solutions at time `t`
6. A solution is "interesting" if at some time moment when it's being tested on test `q`, the percentage shown is exactly `q%`

## Key Insights

1. We need to simulate the testing process over time
2. For each solution, we need to find if there's a moment when it's being tested on test `q` (1 ≤ q ≤ a_i) and the percentage shown is exactly `q%`
3. The percentage at time `t` is `round(100 * m(t) / n)` where `m(t)` is the number of fully tested solutions by time `t`

## Algorithm Steps

1. Simulate the testing schedule using a priority queue to track when each process becomes free
2. For each solution, determine when it starts and when it finishes
3. For each test within a solution (from 1 to a_i), calculate the time when it's being tested
4. At each such time, calculate the current percentage of completed solutions
5. Check if this percentage equals the test number being run

## Implementation Strategy

1. Use a min-heap to track when each process becomes free
2. Process solutions in order
3. For each solution, determine when it starts (when a process becomes free)
4. For each test of this solution (from 1 to a_i), calculate the time when it's being tested
5. At each such time, calculate how many solutions are fully completed
6. Check if the percentage equals the test number

```python
import heapq
import math

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Min heap to store when each process becomes free
    # Initially all processes are free at time 0
    processes = [0] * k
    heapq.heapify(processes)
    
    # Time when each solution starts and ends
    start_time = [0] * n
    end_time = [0] * n
    
    # Process solutions in order
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest available process
        start_time[i] = heapq.heappop(processes)
        end_time[i] = start_time[i] + a[i]
        
        # Add back the process when it finishes
        heapq.heappush(processes, end_time[i])
    
    # Count interesting solutions
    interesting = 0
    
    # For each solution, check each test
    for i in range(n):
        # For each test q from 1 to a[i] of solution i
        for q in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            # Time when solution i is being tested on test q
            test_time = start_time[i] + q
            
            # Calculate how many solutions are fully completed by test_time
            # A solution j is completed if end_time[j] <= test_time
            completed_count = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if end_time[j] <= test_time:
                    completed_count += 1
            
            # Calculate percentage
            percentage = round(100 * completed_count / n)
            
            # Check if this percentage equals the test number q
            if percentage == q:
                interesting += 1
                break  # No need to check further tests for this solution
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the percentage calculation. The problem states that percentage d is calculated as `round(100 * m / n)`, and we need to check if this equals the test number q.

Let me trace through the first example:
- n=2, k=2, a=[49,100]
- Process 1: starts at 0, ends at 49
- Process 2: starts at 0, ends at 100
- Solution 1 is being tested at time 49 on test 50 (since it takes 49 tests)
- At time 49.5, which is during test 50 of solution 1, the percentage is calculated.

Actually, I think I made an error in the test time calculation. The test q happens at time `start_time[i] + q`, not `start_time[i] + q - 1`.

Let me recalculate:

When solution i starts at `start_time[i]`, it runs:
- Test 1 from `start_time[i]` to `start_time[i] + 1`
- Test 2 from `start_time[i] + 1` to `start_time[i] + 2`
- ...
- Test q from `start_time[i] + q - 1` to `start_time[i] + q`

So the test q is being run from time `start_time[i] + q - 1` to `start_time[i] + q`.

Since we only need to check if there exists ANY time when the condition holds, we can check the start of the test (or any point during it).

Let me fix the code:

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Min heap to store when each process becomes free
    processes = [0] * k
    heapq.heapify(processes)
    
    # Time when each solution starts and ends
    start_time = [0] * n
    end_time = [0] * n
    
    # Process solutions in order
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest available process
        start_time[i] = heapq.heappop(processes)
        end_time[i] = start_time[i] + a[i]
        
        # Add back the process when it finishes
        heapq.heappush(processes, end_time[i])
    
    # Count interesting solutions
    interesting = 0
    
    # For each solution, check each test
    for i in range(n):
        # For each test q from 1 to a[i] of solution i
        for q in range(1, a[i] + 1):
            # The time when test q starts
            test_start_time = start_time[i] + q - 1
            # We can check at any point during the test, so we use the start time
            # or just check if the condition holds at that time
            
            # Calculate how many solutions are fully completed by test_start_time
            completed_count = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if end_time[j] <= test_start_time:
                    completed_count += 1
            
            # Calculate percentage
            percentage = round(100 * completed_count / n)
            
            # Check if this percentage equals the test number q
            if percentage == q:
                interesting += 1
                break  # Found one interesting test for this solution
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

            test_start_time = start_time[i] + q - 1

There are $n$ solutions, the $i$-th of them should be tested on $a_i$ tests, testing one solution on one test takes $1$ second. The solutions are judged in the order from $1$ to $n$. There are $k$ testing processes which test solutions simultaneously. Each of them can test at most one solution at a time.

At any time moment $t$ when some testing process is not judging any solution, it takes the first solution from the queue and tests it on each test in increasing order of the test ids. Let this solution have id $i$, then it is being tested on the first test from time moment $t$ till time moment $t + 1$, then on the second test till time moment $t + 2$ and so on. This solution is fully tested at time moment $t + a_i$, and after that the testing process immediately starts testing another solution.

Consider some time moment, let there be exactly $m$ fully tested solutions by this moment. There is a caption "System testing: $d$%" on the page with solutions, where $d$ is calculated as

$$d = round\left(100\cdot\frac{m}{n}\right),$$

where $round(x) = \lfloor{x + 0.5}\rfloor$ is a function which maps every real to the nearest integer.

Vasya calls a submission interesting if there is a time moment (possibly, non-integer) when the solution is being tested on some test $q$, and the caption says "System testing: $q$%". Find the number of interesting solutions.

Please note that in case when multiple processes attempt to take the first submission from the queue at the same moment (for instance, at the initial moment), the order they take the solutions does not matter.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two positive integers $n$ and $k$ ($1 \le n \le 1000$, $1 \le k \le 100$) standing for the number of submissions and the number of testing processes respectively.

The second line contains $n$ positive integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 150$), where $a_i$ is equal to the number of tests the $i$-th submission is to be run on.


-----Output-----

Output the only integer — the number of interesting submissions.


-----Examples-----
Input
2 2
49 100

Output
1

Input
4 2
32 100 33 1

Output
2

Input
14 5
48 19 6 9 50 20 3 42 38 43 36 21 44 6

Output
5



-----Note-----

Consider the first example. At time moment $0$ both solutions start testing. At time moment $49$ the first solution is fully tested, so at time moment $49.5$ the second solution is being tested on the test $50$, and the caption says "System testing: $50$%" (because there is one fully tested solution out of two). So, the second solution is interesting.

Consider the second example. At time moment $0$ the first and the second solutions start testing. At time moment $32$ the first solution is fully tested, the third solution starts testing, the caption says "System testing: $25$%". At time moment $32 + 24.5 = 56.5$ the third solutions is being tested on test $25$, the caption is still the same, thus this solution is interesting. After that the third solution is fully tested at time moment $32 + 33 = 65$, the fourth solution is fully tested at time moment $65 + 1 = 66$. The captions becomes "System testing: $75$%", and at time moment $74.5$ the second solution is being tested on test $75$. So, this solution is also interesting. Overall, there are two interesting solutions.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` solutions that need to be tested, with solution `i` requiring `a_i` tests
2. There are `k` testing processes that work simultaneously
3. Solutions are processed in order from 1 to n
4. When a process is free, it takes the next solution from the queue
5. At any time `t`, if `m` solutions are fully tested, the caption shows "System testing: d%" where `d = round(100 * m / n)`
6. A solution is "interesting" if at some moment when it's being tested on test `q`, the caption shows "q%"

## Key Insights

1. We need to simulate the testing process to determine when each solution starts and finishes
2. For each solution, we need to check if there's a time when it's being tested on test `q` and the caption shows "q%"
3. The caption at time `t` depends on how many solutions are fully tested by time `t`
4. When a solution is being tested on test `q`, it means it's in the process of being tested, so its start time is `start_time` and we're at time `start_time + q - 1`

## Algorithm Steps

1. Simulate the testing process to determine when each solution starts and finishes
2. For each solution, determine all time points when it's being tested on each test
3. For each such time point, calculate the current progress (number of fully tested solutions)
4. Check if the caption percentage equals the test number being tested

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Simulate the testing process**:
   - Use a priority queue to track when each process becomes available
   - Process solutions in order, assigning them to the first available process

2. **For each solution**, determine:
   - When it starts being tested
   - When it finishes being tested
   - For each test `q` from 1 to `a_i`, the time when it's being tested on test `q` is `start_time + q - 1`

3. **At each such time point**, calculate how many solutions are fully tested (by checking completion times)

4. **Check if the caption percentage equals the test number**

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Track when each process becomes available (initially all available at time 0)
    process_avail = [0] * k
    # Track when each solution finishes (start_time + a_i)
    finish_time = [0] * n
    
    # Process solutions in order
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the process that becomes available earliest
        earliest_time = min(process_avail)
        earliest_idx = process_avail.index(earliest_time)
        
        # Solution i starts at earliest_time
        start_time = earliest_time
        finish_time[i] = start_time + a[i]
        
        # Update the process availability
        process_avail[earliest_idx] = start_time + a[i]
    
    # Now check for each solution if it's interesting
    interesting = 0
    
    # For each solution, check each test it's being tested on
    for i in range(n):
        start_time = 0
        # We need to find when this solution actually starts
        # Instead of recalculating, we need to simulate properly
        
        # Let's recalculate properly with a heap-based simulation
        pass
    
    # Let me restart with a cleaner simulation
    
    # Reset and simulate properly
    process_avail = [0] * k
    
    # We need to build the start time array properly
    start_times = [0] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the earliest available process
        earliest_time = min(process_avail)
        earliest_idx = process_avail.index(earliest_time)
        
        start_times[i] = earliest_time
        # The process will finish at start_times[i] + a[i]
        process_avail[earliest_idx] = start_times[i] + a[i]
    
    # Now for each solution, check if any test q has the property that at time start_time + q - 1
    # the caption shows q%
    
    # First, create a list of finish times
    finish_times = [start_times[i] + a[i] for i in range(n)]
    
    # For each solution, check all test points
    interesting = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        start = start_times[i]
        a_i = a[i]
        
        # For each test q from 1 to a_i
        for q in range(1, a_i + 1):
            time = start + q - 1  # time when test q is being executed
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by this time
            m = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if finish_times[j] <= time:
                    m += 1
            
            # Calculate the caption percentage
            d = round(100 * m / n)
            
            # Check if this matches q
            if d == q:
                interesting += 1
                break  # Once we find one, this solution is interesting
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Simulate the process using a min heap for process availability
    # Each heap element is (next_available_time, process_index)
    process_avail = [0] * k
    
    # Start times for each solution
    start_times = [0] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the process that becomes available earliest
        min_time = min(process_avail)
        min_idx = process_avail.index(min_time)
        
        start_times[i] = min_time
        # This process will be free at start_times[i] + a[i]
        process_avail[min_idx] = start_times[i] + a[i]
    
    # Now check for interesting solutions
    interesting = 0
    
    # Finish times for each solution
    finish_times = [start_times[i] + a[i] for i in range(n)]
    
    for i in range(n):
        start = start_times[i]
        tests = a[i]
        
        # Check each test q from 1 to tests
        for q in range(1, tests + 1):
            current_time = start + q - 1
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by current_time
            m = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if finish_times[j] <= current_time:
                    m += 1
            
            # Calculate caption percentage
            d = round(100 * m / n)
            
            # If the caption shows q%, then this solution is interesting
            if d == q:
                interesting += 1
                break  # No need to check further tests for this solution
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
```

import heapq

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Simulate the process using a min heap for process availability
    # Each heap element is (next_available_time, process_index)
    process_avail = [0] * k
    
    # Start times for each solution
    start_times = [0] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the process that becomes available earliest
        min_time = min(process_avail)
        min_idx = process_avail.index(min_time)
        
        start_times[i] = min_time
        # This process will be free at start_times[i] + a[i]
        process_avail[min_idx] = start_times[i] + a[i]
    
    # Now check for interesting solutions
    interesting = 0
    
    # Finish times for each solution
    finish_times = [start_times[i] + a[i] for i in range(n)]
    
    for i in range(n):
        start = start_times[i]
        tests = a[i]
        
        # Check each test q from 1 to tests
        for q in range(1, tests + 1):
            current_time = start + q - 1
            
            # Count how many solutions are fully tested by current_time
            m = 0
            for j in range(n):
                if finish_times[j] <= current_time:
                    m += 1
            
            # Calculate caption percentage
            d = round(100 * m / n)
            
            # If the caption shows q%, then this solution is interesting
            if d == q:
                interesting += 1
                break  # No need to check further tests for this solution
    
    return interesting

print(solve())
